Strike force prosecutor Drew Pitt argued that bail should not be reduced because Isgro, he alleged, was “threatening to harm” his former bodyguard, David Michael Smith, now a cooperating witness.* Such threats should be taken seriously, Pitt said, because Isgro had “influence with people in organized crime.” Pitt charged that Isgro shared a $1 million money-laundering operation with convicted mafioso Joe “Piney” Armone. Don Re mocked these assertions, and Magistrate Brown did reduce Isgro’s bail, but only to $300,000. Isgro was nonetheless able to post bond that evening. He subsequently entered a plea of not guilty; so did Anderson and Monka.

Free for the time being, Isgro faced up to two hundred years in jail and $1.5 million in fines if he lost his case. Unlike Tashjian and Craig, Isgro had been hit with RICO, the tough racketeering statute that had been used successfully against mobsters and Wall Street miscreants.

Tashjian and Craig, the first men to be convicted under the payola statute since Kenny Gamble pleaded nolo contendere in 1976, were given light sentences—a tip-off that both had assisted in the case against Isgro. Craig drew five years’ probation, a $60,000 fine, and one hundred hours of community service. Tashjian was sentenced to sixty days in a halfway house, three years’ probation, a $100,000 fine, and five hundred hours of community service. Judge Rymer said she had planned stiffer punishment for Tashjian but was moved by his tearful seven-minute plea, in which he claimed to have overcome an addiction to alcohol and cocaine. “Since my indictment,” Tashjian said, “even though my wife and I have gone through hell, I can honestly say these have been the best years of my life because of my change in spirituality.”

The same month that Isgro and his codefendants were indicted, the government’s campaign against indie promotion practices spread to another front. On November 20, 1989, the U.S. Attorney’s Office in Memphis filed mail fraud and payola charges against Howard Goodman, a local independent promoter. Goodman was well known in the industry as a subcontractor for a member of the Network. At his trial the following June, four radio programmers, including two former employees of WQID in Biloxi, testified that Goodman had bribed them with cash, typically stuffed in a birthday card and mailed to their homes. One witness testified that his bribes totaled somewhere between $50,000 and $100,000 over three and a half years. Goodman was found guilty on all counts on July 2, after less than ninety minutes of deliberation by the jury.

The Goodman conviction left the industry nervous. Did this mean there was now a coordinated, national effort against illegal practices in the music business? It meant nothing of the kind. The Goodman case had originated from leads provided by ill-fated Los Angeles strike force attorney Marvin Rudnick, after one of Goodman’s birthday-card recipients willingly revealed himself to the L.A. prosecutor. “I would have tried to convict Goodman on a deal and get him to testify against higher-ups,” Rudnick said in retrospect. Unfortunately, the IRS took charge of the matter and referred it to the Memphis authorities, who had no such ambitions. After Goodman was convicted, the case was closed.

For opponents of indie promotion, there were still some hopeful signs. True, Rudnick had been fired from the strike force, and the new government team had nearly blown the payola case by attempting, in Judge Rymer’s view, improperly to coerce a guilty plea from Valerie Tashjian. But after that near-fiasco, the Justice Department had sent in a veteran Washington prosecutor, William Lynch, to oversee the Los Angeles investigation. It was Lynch who had brought the indictment against Isgro, and now the odds against the promotion man appeared daunting.

For starters, the government’s witness list included not only two of Isgro’s top employees—Ralph Tashjian and bodyguard David Smith—but also his former accountant, Dennis DiRicco. Granted, none of these witnesses were impeccable, least of all DiRicco, an ex-IRS agent who had recently been convicted in three cases in San Francisco, on charges ranging from conspiring to sell cocaine to various tax offenses. But the strike force had further ammunition against Isgro. It had given immunity to four former program directors who were prepared to detail the payoffs they had allegedly received from Isgro’s promotion company. In addition, Isgro could take little comfort in the fact that the federal judge chosen to preside over his case, James Ideman, had a reputation for being tough on defendants.

The first witnesses in United States v. Joseph Isgro et al. were called to the stand the final week of August 1990. Ralph Tashjian wasted no time incriminating his former boss. He testified that whenever he found a programmer willing to take payola, Isgro’s standard response was, “Do what you gotta do.” When Tashjian needed cash for a bribe, he added, “I would submit a check request” to Isgro’s office staff, “then deposit the check in my corporate checking account and write myself a check for cash. I would then take the cash, put it in a Federal Express envelope, and send it.”

George Crowell, the former programmer of KIQQ in Los Angeles, told the court that in 1980, he and Isgro had set an initial payment of $750 for each record added to the KIQQ playlist. The alleged arrangement was worth more than $100,000 a year to Crowell. Every Tuesday, he said, “I would call Joe and tell him which of his records got on the air.” Then, one of Isgro’s employees “would meet me at Martoni’s or the Jolly Roger,” two restaurants in Los Angeles. “I’d meet them in the men’s room, collect the money in a record envelope, and go back to the bar.” Prosecutor Lynch asked Crowell if he and Isgro knew that the payments were a violation of federal law. Crowell answered that Isgro had once told him, “We’re both big boys, and we’ll have to take the heat.”

Robert Brulte, who programmed KAMZ in El Paso, and Edward Carey of KMGX in Fresno both acknowledged receiving cocaine from Ralph Tashjian; Brulte also got cash. Another Fresno programmer, Johnny Lee Walker of KYNO, said he had already been taking payola from Memphis-based indie promoter Howard Goodman when Tashjian lured him away with a higher cash bid. Later, when Walker developed what he termed “a problem with cocaine,” he persuaded Tashjian to deliver small quantities of the drug, usually an eighth of an ounce, “inside a plastic cassette case.” This admission must have been a difficult one for Walker, considering that he had been cochairman of the Fresno County “Just Say No” program.

The testimony from David Smith was no less dramatic. He claimed that Dennis DiRicco, the former accountant, had been laundering large sums of money for Isgro. Smith said that beginning in 1984, he made frequent trips to visit DiRicco at the San Francisco airport, each time bringing back between $60,000 and $100,000 in cash in a briefcase. Smith and Isgro would then count the money, stuff portions of it into record sleeves, and mail the records to radio stations. The bodyguard also claimed to have seen Isgro deliver cash payments as large as $30,000 to indie promoter Fred DiSipio. Smith further testified that on three occasions, he watched as Isgro handed over bundles of cash, including a payment of at least $10,000, to Gambino family underboss Joe “Piney” Armone.

After four days of such damning testimony, William Lynch and his assistant counsel, Drew Pitt, must have smelled a victory. A cruel surprise awaited them.

On August 25, one of the defense attorneys procured a transcript of statements that government witness Dennis DiRicco had made on the stand at his own drug-related trial. Many of DiRicco’s statements conflicted dramatically with his testimony before the grand jury that indicted Isgro. For example, DiRicco told the grand jury he had laundered money for Isgro, while at his own trial, he had flatly denied that charge.

Arguing that the government had been obliged to make the grand jury aware of DiRicco’s prior conflicting testimony, the defense team moved to have the case dismissed. The government was also accused of possessing the San Francisco trial transcript, yet deliberately withholding it from the defense team. Prosecutor Lynch had indeed obtained a copy of the transcript as early as April 1989, but denied any “intentional and conscious effort to hide evidence” from the defense. The government also contended that it was not required by law to introduce DiRicco’s trial testimony to the grand jury.

To the shock of the strike force attorneys, Judge Ideman gave great weight to the defense motion. On August 29, he sternly rebuked the government in open court and threatened to throw out the entire indictment “with prejudice”—meaning that the case could not be retried unless the Federal Court of Appeals found Ideman to be in error. According to one courtroom observer that day, strike force attorney William Lynch “looked as though he had just been whacked with a two-by-four.” DiRicco had not yet even been called as a witness at the Isgro trial—and wasn’t it obvious from all the other testimony that the case did not hinge on him alone? Furthermore, how could the government “withhold” a public document? Dismissal with prejudice was the most extreme remedy possible—and Ideman was supposed to be a prosecutor’s judge. What was going on here?

Apparently Ideman believed the government had deliberately tried to mislead the defense. On September 4, he ruled that prosecutors “repeatedly denied the existence” of the trial transcript, for fear that it would damage their case. With heavy sarcasm, Ideman also debunked the government’s assertion that DiRicco’s prior testimony did not impeach him as a witness against Isgro. “Having been caught with a smoking pistol,” the judge said, “the government now denies that the pistol smoked.” Ideman then proceeded to make good on his threat. The government’s case—four years and several million dollars in the making—was dismissed with prejudice. Joe Isgro, Ray Anderson, and Jeffrey Monka walked out of the district court as free men by dint of what the judge termed “outrageous government misconduct.”

The irony of what had just happened was not lost on supporters of Marvin Rudnick, the deposed strike force attorney who had launched the payola case. Rudnick had been removed because his superiors supposedly feared that he was a “loose cannon.” Now his successors had been reprimanded by two different judges for misconduct. Meanwhile, exactly one month after the Isgro case was thrown out, a House committee would at long last exonerate Rudnick, pronouncing him “a dedicated, effective, and hard-working prosecutor whose honesty and integrity were beyond reproach.”

Ambushed by reporters outside the courtroom immediately after Ideman’s ruling, prosecutor Lynch insisted that the judge had erred and vowed to file an appeal. To do so, he would first need the approval of the solicitor general. Within a few weeks, Lynch got that approval. As of March 1991, the appeal was still pending, and many observers felt that the government had a good shot. The record business, however, has already rendered its verdict: The government is incapable of sending any major music industry figure to jail. (The one notable exception, Morris Levy, died before serving a day of his ten-year sentence.)

It should, therefore, come as no surprise that the independent promotion system of the early eighties is back in full swing. Nowadays, the price of promoting a Top 40 single typically runs into the six figures, just as it did before NBC News exposed the Network in February 1986. Most of the major record companies are once again hiring the top indies directly, although the cost is still largely recouped from the artists’ royalties. The Network lives on into the nineties.

As for Joe Isgro, after his indictment was thrown out, he immediately plunged back into the entertainment business. The Jimmy Hoffa movie project got the green light from 20th Century-Fox. And there were plenty of other ventures to occupy his time. In February 1991, he acquired a New Jersey recording studio called City Lights and expressed plans to expand it into a new label, Highway 33 Records. The previous owner of City Lights was now in no position to run it. He was Gaetano “the Big Guy” Vastola, the mafioso who was nailed in the same extortion case that brought down Morris Levy. Of course, there was nothing to prevent Joe Isgro from doing business with a felon such as Vastola. Isgro, after all, had never been convicted of breaking the law.


* At the moment, Smith was stewing in state prison. He had got in a barroom brawl the previous August.





20

WALTER’S FALL




FOR nearly a decade, the notion of the independent label had been largely a myth. By the eighties, a better term for record companies such as Chrysalis, A&M, Island, Virgin, and the like would have been “dependent” labels—dependent on one of the six majors for the distribution and, in a number of cases, manufacturing. With the sale of CBS Records to Sony in February 1988 for the unprecedented sum of $2 billion, it was only a question of time before such labels would acknowledge their subordinate status, take advantage of a hot market, and sell themselves to the majors lock, stock, and barrel.

The first post-CBS acquisition was more significant as an end-of-an-era story than for its impact on the music industry. In mid-1988 Motown was purchased by MCA and some outside investors for $61 million. The low price—less than Motown’s annual sales in its heyday—illustrated that there was little left to the label apart from its esteemed R&B catalog, and even that had largely been milked dry.

Nineteen-eighty-nine proved to be a bumper year for purchases by the majors. The buying spree made an even richer man of attorney Allen Grubman, who had discovered the joyful secret of investment banking—it is lucrative to be standing by when a lot of money changes hands. Grubman by now seemed to represent almost every label in America. “I’ve come of age,” he boasted. In January 1989 Grubman helped Thorn-EMI consummate its $295 million purchase of one of his clients, SBK, a large music publishing house and custom record label. Though neither Grubman nor his firm did any of the legal paperwork, he pocketed an estimated $3 million as a finder’s fee.

Two months after inking the SBK deal, EMI paid $75 million for 50 percent of Chrysalis Records, with an option to buy the other 50 percent. Grubman was the attorney for Chrysalis. He was also the lawyer for David Geffen, who, a short time before EMI stepped in, had bought 10 percent of the public holding company that owned Chrysalis Records, apparently in hopes of acquiring the label. Around this time, Grubman fell out of favor with Geffen, perhaps because Geffen was disappointed at losing an opportunity to acquire Chrysalis. Whatever the cause of the rupture, it was soon healed, and Grubman would take special care from then on to remain in Geffen’s good graces.

As EMI digested its quarry, another European-owned distributor, PolyGram, prepared for an even bigger feast. In July 1989 PolyGram bought Island Records for $272 million, mostly in cash. The sale, which included music publishing, was facilitated by Island’s attorney, Allen Grubman. By September, PolyGram had entered into an agreement to acquire A&M for $460 million in cash, the biggest deal up to that time for an independent label. (For once, Grubman did not get a piece of the action. A&M had relied on another firm.)

Unfortunately for Dick Asher, PolyGram’s acquisition of A&M and Island marked the beginning of the end of his presidency. He did not participate in the negotiations, and he did not agree with the brain trust in Europe that the mergers made sense at the prices being paid. Meanwhile, there was growing tension between Asher and the PolyGram board over his compensation. When he took the PolyGram job in 1985, Asher was given a bonus plan pegged to the U.S. division’s earnings but, to its subsequent regret, the PolyGram board forgot to put a ceiling on the amount Asher could be awarded. At the time, it may not have seemed an issue to PolyGram. The Dutch company had lost money year after year in the United States; in 1985 it made a measly $3 million. Under Asher—thanks in large measure to the success of Bon Jovi and the heavy-metal craze—the company began to net $15 to $20 million a year. Asher was suddenly taking home an annual paycheck in the “healthy seven figures,” outearning even the chairman of N. V. Philips, PolyGram’s parent company. Quite by accident, Asher had made a big score of his own.

Asher was forced to resign in November 1989 because, he said, the board could not resolve the compensation dilemma. PolyGram head David Fine, who had replaced Jan Timmer, gave Billboard a different account of the divorce. The bonus issue “never even arose,” he said. “It was purely a question of chemistry and performance.” Considering that PolyGram’s profits had soared in five years under Asher, the gripe about performance was hard to figure, unless it reflected Dick’s failure to break new artists, rather than just the ones he had inherited. The problem of bad chemistry, however, was not a new one for Dick.

At least Asher’s fall was cushioned by his newfound wealth, and he accepted the loss of his job with equanimity. “I have ideas of teaching,” he said a short time after leaving PolyGram. “That’s what I wanted to do before my folks persuaded me to go to law school. I’d like to lead a less structured life. Maybe,” he added with a sigh, “it’s all for the best.”

Though Asher had taken his place alongside Yetnikoff in the record industry’s multimillionaire club, neither of them could hold a candle to David Geffen. By early 1990, Geffen Records was, with the exception of Virgin, the last of the large independent labels yet to be acquired by one of the majors. It had long been assumed that if Geffen Records ever were sold, the buyer would be Warner Communications. For more than twenty years, Warner chairman Steve Ross had acted like a surrogate father to David Geffen. But the relationship had soured when Warner merged with Time Inc. in 1989 to form Time Warner. Geffen placed a premium on knowing everyone else’s business—he was capable, it was said, of handling a hundred phone calls in a day—yet he had been kept in the dark about the merger talks. Worse, Steve Ross finally made an offer for Geffen Records that Geffen found insulting.

For several weeks, Geffen entertained bids from other potential buyers, including Ross’s bitter enemy, Paramount Communications. Finally, it appeared that the high bidder was EMI, which made an offer worth about $675 million. But there were tax problems, and Geffen did not feel comfortable with EMI management. Instead, in March 1990, Geffen announced that he was selling his company to MCA Corporation for ten million shares of MCA common stock, worth at the time about $540 million. Had the merger taken place several months earlier, Geffen would have ended up reporting to his old nemesis, MCA Records chief Irving Azoff. But Azoff had quit that job in September 1989 and resurfaced as the head of Giant Records, a new label bankrolled by Warner.

Geffen’s MCA shares amounted to 12 percent of the company, making him an even bigger stockholder than the studio’s aging chairman, Lew Wasserman, though with more restricted voting rights. Considering that Geffen Records had existed for only a decade, and that in its best year the company had earned $40 million—well, it appeared that MCA was paying a lot of goodwill to win the services of David Geffen. In 1980 he had put his net worth at about $30 million; in 1990, it nudged toward the magic one-billion mark.

“The entire music industry is jealous of David Geffen,” said one leading music business figure. “He’s the greatest success in the history of show business. People don’t understand how he does it. What did he sell MCA? He sold them one superstar group [Guns N’ Roses], five other good acts of varying degrees, a catalog of no more than one hundred and fifty albums, and contracts with some first-rate music executives. As far as I know, that’s all he sold them. This is the greatest salesman of our lifetime.”

During the course of 1990, however, Geffen took a large paper loss, as the price of MCA stock dipped further and further. Part of the drop was simply the result of a bear market, but it also reflected pessimism on the part of arbitrageurs. Lew Wasserman had never found it in him to sell MCA, having scotched a number of merger deals in the past. Wall Street was apparently betting that Wasserman, old as he was, would continue to resist a takeover. Geffen clearly was betting otherwise. As usual, Geffen won the bet. In November 1990 MCA was sold to Matsushita, a large Japanese manufacturer of entertainment hardware. Geffen’s ten million shares became $660 million in cash.

In the process, MCA Records passed into Japanese hands. Now, five of the six major record distribution companies were foreign owned. Only time would tell whether this made a difference in the way the American music business was run.



By the middle of 1989, people who saw Walter Yetnikoff agreed that he looked terrible. His two-year-old marriage to Cynthia Slamar was all but over. The belief that Slamar would help Walter clean up his act after his tumultuous years with Lynda Emon had turned out to be a fantasy. Walter Yetnikoff was an alcoholic. For years, he had failed to come to grips with this disturbing fact, even though he kept a fully stocked bar in his office and was capable of downing several bourbons before noon. Finally, by August, his body could take no more abuse. Yetnikoff told his superiors at Sony that he was checking into the Hazelden Clinic, a center for drug and alcohol dependence in Minnesota.

When he emerged on September 10, Walter Yetnikoff was a new man—or so he claimed. To be sure, he was sober now. Even more important, he said, the weeks at Hazelden had been a time of spiritual reawakening, and he was finally at peace with himself. Sadly, nothing could have been further from the truth. Yetnikoff was about to embark on the record industry’s version of Appointment in Samarra, seemingly hellbent on self-destruction. Except that, in a departure from the novel, his own decline would be touched off by abstinence. “Think of it this way,” said a former CBS Records executive, straining for an explanation. “I’m sure you’ve been in plenty of clubs that have sleaziness or shittiness to them, but at night, when the music’s on, and the lights are doing their shtick, it’s sort of okay? But if you go back to that same club at three o’clock in the afternoon, and you see it for what it is, you ain’t too thrilled? I think that maybe when Walter saw who he was in the light of day, he was not too thrilled. He’s a very unhappy man.”

Whatever the case, Walter was suddenly crazier, not calmer, and over the next year, people would wonder why Sony had been so indulgent for so long. Perhaps Sony was reluctant to admit it had made a $20 million mistake—the estimated size of Yetnikoff’s sign-on bonus. Or perhaps it had simply bought into Yetnikoff’s rock-and-roll-rabbi mythology—that the screaming fits and crude behavior somehow made him more appealing to the top recording stars. He was, after all, the self-proclaimed impresario of “schmoozing, schmingling, and bingling.” Who could argue with that? Maybe it wasn’t the Japanese way, but this was the American pop music business.

One thing was certain: By the time Walter’s boozing drove him into the sick ward, he had already proven a liability to Sony in ways that might have sunk a lesser man. No sooner had Sony acquired CBS Records when CBS Inc., in a counterclaim to a lawsuit, accused Walter and members of his management team of having “improperly depressed” profits.* And over the next year, CBS Records had fallen a distant second to Warner in domestic market share.

In spite of all this, by mid-1989, Sony was prepared to give Yetnikoff added responsibility. He had been made the point man in Sony’s campaign to acquire an American movie studio, which would report through him. Just as the purchase of CBS Records had been part of a master plan to sell DAT hardware, Sony needed a film catalog to abet the eventual launch of HDTV, or high definition television. Walter had his own agenda. He had got a taste of Hollywood as an executive producer of Ruthless People and was all too aware that a dual influence in movies and records had enhanced the power of Geffen and Azoff.

At first, Sony held talks with MGM/UA, but those negotiations quickly collapsed. Soon after that, however, Sony set its sights on Columbia Pictures, a once-vaunted studio that had fallen on hard times. Its previous owner, the Coca-Cola Company, had recently spun off 51 percent of Columbia to private shareholders. By the summer of 1989, Sony had more or less agreed to purchase all the stock of Columbia Pictures for $3.4 billion, but it was not quite ready to cut the check. Sony would have to find someone to replace the departing head of Columbia, and that person had yet to be found. For a while, Sony held intense discussions with superagent Michael Ovitz, but he ultimately declined the job.

When Yetnikoff emerged from Hazelden, he presented Sony with the solution to its dilemma. Walter suggested that Sony make independent producers Peter Guber and Jon Peters the coheads of Columbia Pictures. For years, Walter had been friends with Peters, who had gained entry to Hollywood first as the hairdresser and then as the lover and manager of Barbra Streisand. In partnership with Peter Guber, who had run the latter half of Casablanca Record and FilmWorks, Peters was now one of the hottest producers in the business. Guber and Peters’s credits included one of the top-grossing movies of all time, Batman, and they had been executive producers of the hit Rain Man. The team also produced some bombs, including Clan of the Cave Bear and Who’s That Girl? The two men made it abundantly clear to Walter that they were prepared to accept.

It remained for Yetnikoff’s bosses, who had no experience in Hollywood, to give their consent. Guber flew to New York and met with Michael Schulhof, Sony’s U.S. chief and the only American on its board of directors. Schulhof’s approval was essential, and not merely because of his corporate rank. He and Sony chairman Akio Morita were extremely close. Like Morita, Mickey Schulhof was a trained scientist, having earned a Ph.D. in physics from Brandeis (his doctoral thesis was on neutron scattering in anti-ferromagnets). Schulhof and Guber discovered that they, too, had something in common—both men were amateur radio operators. On such subtleties are many corporate decisions made. Within a week of Yetnikoff s release from Hazelden, Guber and Peters had entered into an agreement to take charge of Columbia Pictures.

It fell mostly to Walter to negotiate a compensation package for his friends. The contract he helped to draft was outrageous even by Hollywood standards. Sony agreed to pay $200 million, a large premium over market value, for Guber-Peters Inc., which produced TV game shows, Guber and Peters owned 28 percent of that company. They also got yearly salaries of $2.7 million apiece, $50 million in deferred compensation, and an 8.08 percent share of Columbia’s equity appreciation. (“Why 8.08 percent?” Guber reportedly asked at one point. “So you’ll remember me when this is over,” said Yetnikoff.)

The deal soon became even more costly for Sony. Guber and Peters had just signed a five-year contract to have their films released through Warner Bros. When it discovered that the two producers had turned around and agreed to run Columbia Pictures, Time Warner filed a $1 billion suit for breach of contract. The defendants in the suit were Sony, Guber-Peters, Walter Yetnikoff, and Michael Schulhof. Sony and Guber-Peters counter-sued Time Warner for trying to block the deal.

Deeply embarrassed by the litigation, Sony dispatched Schulhof to hammer out a settlement. Yetnikoff was excluded from the negotiations because, at an early meeting with Time Warner executives to discuss whether the suit could be avoided in the first place, he had been anything but a diplomat. At one point, he so goaded and irritated Warner Bros, chairman Bob Daly that the studio boss responded with a Bronx cheer. After the meeting broke up, Yetnikoff reportedly told Time Warner’s outside counsel, Arthur Liman—one of the nation’s most respected attorneys—that he could go fuck himself. This was not exactly evidence of Walter’s spiritual rebirth.

Worse, in the days that followed, Walter badmouthed Time Warner chairman Steve Ross with an emotional fervor he had not exhibited since his blood feud with Larry Tisch. He made the dispute even more personal by refusing to let Ross’s wife, Courtney Sale, use a Michael Jackson song in a movie she was producing about the life of Quincy Jones. When Ross complained about Yetnikoff’s foul language and lack of decorum, Walter was unrepentant. “Sony never heard me talk like this before?” he demanded. “And as if Sony’s gonna control what I have to say anyway. They like the way I talk. They paid for part of this shtick and theatrics stuff.”

Perhaps Schulhof was overeager to come to terms with Time Warner, because the consensus was that he gave away the store. The suit was dropped, and Time Warner agreed to release Guber and Peters from their contract, but Sony gave up, among other things, 50 percent of CBS’s lucrative record club—a prize that, under normal circumstances, might have cost Time Warner $300 million. The deal was so terrible that, according to Vanity Fair, the wags in Hollywood were calling it “Pearl Harbor revenged.”

But Walter’s dream had apparently come true. In October 1989 he was named head of a steering committee that would oversee both Columbia Pictures and the records group. He was now a macher in the movie business, and he reveled in it. “Will I keep sticking my nose in?” he gleefully asked a reporter. “Yeah. Can I call Guber and Peters and say, ‘You told me two weeks ago you were supposed to have a meeting; what the fuck do you think you’re doing here? I want that meeting!’? Yeah. But I’ll get a straight answer, not because of any corporate type of thing, but because I know these guys. I’ll tell them to fuck off. That’s why I like them.”

It did not work out that way. Guber, whose personal coffers had been filled to overflowing by Yetnikoff, made it clear to the Japanese that he did not see a role for Walter in Columbia Pictures. “Our understanding had always been that we would deal directly with Sony,” Guber later explained. “There never was any committee.”

Yetnikoff made a bad situation worse by trying to force Mickey Schulhof to back him against Guber. A close business acquaintance claims that Yetnikoff became abusive when he did not get his way. Schulhof had always liked Walter, but his patience was wearing thin. There was already tension between the two men because during the settlement talks with Time Warner, Walter had given Schulhof rude advice from the sidelines. “Walter was calling him ‘Mickola,’ ” a longtime Yetnikoff confidant recalled. “He would say, Mickola—watch my lips. No, Mickola. Don’t give them that. I’m giving you ten minutes to call Warner and say so-and-so. Otherwise, I’m going to quit and walk out with the artists.” Insulting Schulhof in this manner was tantamount to insulting Morita.

Such behavior was foolish, though not out of character. Yetnikoff had always mouthed off to his bosses. The one thing he had never done was damage his relationships with artists. But almost from the day that Sony bought CBS, Walter’s attitude toward the talent seemed to change. He was laying waste to relationships that he had carefully cultivated for years. Perhaps because he had achieved his big score, he simply no longer cared. How else could one explain, for example, his sudden nastiness toward Bruce Springsteen and the artist’s manager, Jon Landau? In 1988 Springsteen agreed to take part in a tour to raise money for Amnesty International. Yetnikoff was incensed. Amnesty International had a bias against the state of Israel, he insisted, and Springsteen must not participate in the tour. When Springsteen did so anyway, Walter refused to take Jon Landau’s calls. In the meantime, Yetnikoff disparaged both Springsteen and his manager in public, and made vulgar remarks about the singer’s divorce. “He took a fourteen-year relationship and trashed it for irrational reasons,” said a source close to Springsteen.

If Walter seemed increasingly incapable of directing the record division, the vacuum was amply filled by his trusted number two man, Tommy Mottola. Within a month of replacing Al Teller as president of CBS Records U.S., Mottola began to assemble a staff loyal to him and all but unknown to Yetnikoff. He hired Atlantic Records’ Dave Glew as general manager of Epic; tapped a PolyGram vice president, Mel Ilberman, as his executive assistant; and brought in Michele Anthony, an entertainment lawyer, as a senior VP. Later, he recruited Don Ienner, a promotion man who had risen to Arista’s number two spot, as head of Columbia.

Walter had allowed Tommy to restaff the upper echelons of the company with his own soldiers, and to some, that seemed like a surefire recipe for a palace coup. Clearly, it did not appear that way to Walter. He had got rid of Asher and then Teller because he had perceived them as threatening. Surely, Tommy Mottola, whom Lynda Emon referred to as Walter’s “personal valet,” would never be disloyal. There were onlookers, however, who believed that Tommy would turn on Walter in the blink of an eye. The late Morris Levy, for instance, had once considered Mottola a friend. But after Levy was convicted—about the same time that Tommy left artist management to join CBS—Mottola stopped taking his calls. To Levy, this was unforgivable. “Mottola is a no-talent mover-upper,” Morris concluded. “He’s a user.”

Walter also seemed to believe that Allen Grubman would never forget that Yetnikoff had “created” the attorney—and just to make sure, he continually reminded Grubman of the fact. Unfortunately, Walter had done his job too well. Grubman was now so firmly established that a threat from Yetnikoff no longer sent him scurrying for the Valium bottle. Allen had done all he could to regain the good graces of David Geffen, much to Walter’s irritation. When Yetnikoff ordered Grubman to stop dealing with Geffen so much, the attorney was not inclined to listen. Who needed Walter? As Grubman put it to a friend, Walter had lost his “fail-safe mechanism.” If Yetnikoff was overthrown at CBS, Grubman could not lose. He represented the three top people below Walter: Tommy Mottola, Don Ienner, and CBS Records International head Bob Summer. Rumor had it that Walter had told Allen, I’ll do you a favor—I won’t destroy you for now. And that Allen had shot back, Thanks, I’ll do you the same favor. In any case, by mid-1990, the friendship was over. “I’m a little bored with Allen,” Yetnikoff told reporter Fred Goodman. “Bored with ‘My fee’ being his first comment, and then ‘Let’s do what’s right’ being the last.”

Apart from his falling-out with Grubman, Walter made an even more tragic mistake—he aroused the rage of David Geffen. Here was the best evidence to support the theory that Walter was consciously committing professional hara-kiri. Geffen was vengeful and used to getting what he wanted. And shortly after Walter’s release from Hazelden, what Geffen wanted most was a Michael Jackson single for the movie Days of Thunder, since his record company was going to put out the soundtrack album. When Jackson declined to write an original song for the movie, Geffen agreed to settle for an outtake from the Bad album—a cover of John Lennon’s “Come Together.”

The trouble was, Walter did not agree that Geffen could have the recording. Worse, his method of delivering the bad news was crude even by Walter’s standards. Suffused as he was with macho bluster, Yetnikoff had occasionally made sarcastic references over the years about Geffen’s bisexuality, but never to his face.* Now, on the phone with Geffen Records president Eric Eisner, Yetnikoff was screaming about Geffen’s attempts to secure the Jackson single, and the essence of his tirade was a rather fanciful claim that Geffen obviously was performing oral sex on a certain male executive at Twentieth-Century Fox. (This was, at best, a non sequitur, considering that Days of Thunder was a Paramount picture.) Walter also told Eisner that he would compensate Geffen handsomely if he would give Yetnikoff’s new girlfriend lessons in fellatio. Geffen was not amused in the slightest. This was war.

Geffen began to explore a course of action that, if successful, would surely damage Walter’s professional standing. If he couldn’t get a Michael Jackson single, could he get Michael Jackson himself? As Geffen was well aware, the singer’s recording pact with CBS had been negotiated under California law, which provides that contracts can be abrogated after seven years—an amount of time that had already expired in Jackson’s case. And Geffen certainly had set the stage for making a pass at the singer. For more than a decade, Geffen had served as an unpaid business adviser to Jackson. When a reporter tried to compliment him for schmoozing Jackson in this manner, his reply was pure Geffen: “Well, wait a second, I don’t know if that’s exactly the right way to look at it. You mean, Michael Jackson’s managed to get one of the smartest guys in the entertainment business to give him free advice—it’s not exactly my schmoozing him, he’s probably schmoozing me. I’m doing him a favor, he’s not doing me a favor.”

Soon after Yetnikoff’s ill-advised tirade, Geffen did Jackson the favor of pointing out how Walter had taken advantage of him. CBS, he noted, had made money from the singer’s feature-length video, Moonwalker—but Jackson had lost $16 million on the project. Walter had left himself vulnerable to such criticism because, as with Springsteen, he had failed to keep up his friendship with Jackson.

The singer’s former manager, Frank Dileo, a diehard Yetnikoff loyalist, might have saved the day for Walter. But he had been let go in early 1989, partly at Geffen’s urging. Dileo had not been replaced. Instead, his role was filled by Jackson’s lawyer of more than a decade, John Branca. Branca was very much Walter’s man, and therefore an obstacle between Geffen and Jackson. Geffen had never had much love for Branca.

Despite Branca’s presence, however, it seemed that Jackson was increasingly willing to take his cue from Geffen. In mid-1990, for example, Geffen asked Jackson to appear at the opening of Universal Studios’ new tour in Florida. (Universal was part of Geffen’s parent company, MCA.) Uncharacteristically, Jackson agreed. But then, Disney chairman Michael Eisner asked Jackson to reconsider. Jackson had starred in Captain EO, a three-dimensional movie shown at Disney theme parks, and another such film was on the drawing board. If Jackson appeared at the Universal tour, Eisner warned, there would be no new movie. On June 3, 1990, Jackson was admitted to a California hospital, complaining of chest pains. He thereby missed the Universal tour. A source close to Jackson claims the malady was either a fake or brought on by the stress of not wishing to disappoint Geffen.

Then again, perhaps the real source of stress concerned John Branca. On June 19, only a week after his release from the hospital, Jackson sent Branca a termination notice. The letter was promptly rescinded, but by early July, Branca was let go by Jackson once and for all. Branca later learned that Geffen had convinced Jackson that the attorney was “too close” to Walter Yetnikoff. Geffen also noted that Jackson had sold 77 million albums for CBS—more than all its other top acts combined—and suggested that his royalty rate should be higher. And he portrayed Branca as greedy. Branca had got himself 5 percent of the profit of Jackson’s Bad tour, plus a piece of the singer’s lucrative deal with Pepsi-Cola. Now, Branca wanted an override on publishing as well.

Branca, however, felt he had done pretty well for his client. Jackson’s royalty was 41 percent of wholesale, plus a nonrecoupable advance of $3 million an album, and a recoupable advance of $15 million. Springsteen, by contrast, got a 38 percent royalty, a recoupable advance of $2 to $3 million, and no nonrecoupable advance. Moreover, Branca believed he deserved to be cut in on Jackson’s publishing because he devoted so much time to the singer that the rest of his practice was suffering. Clearly, it was Geffen’s brief to Jackson that won the day. (Geffen was not omnipotent, however. In the wake of his firing by Jackson, Branca picked up two dozen new clients, including Aerosmith, one of Geffen’s top acts.)

Jackson replaced Branca with a manager and three attorneys. The manager, Sandy Gallin, happened to be one of Geffen’s closest friends. For publishing, Jackson now used Lee Phillips—Geffen’s West Coast attorney. For litigation, he hired Bert Fields, who was also Geffen’s litigator. For records, he retained Allen Grubman. If Jackson had bought the argument that Branca was too close to Yetnikoff, he had obviously not thought matters through. Grubman had made a career out of being close to label presidents, and now he represented three of the four top managers of CBS Records. Granted, Yetnikoff was not one of them. According to Rolling Stone, when he learned that Jackson had hired Grubman, “Walter went crazy.”

Rumor of Michael Jackson’s possible defection to Geffen Records had presumably reached Sony’s ears by now. As it turned out, however, Geffen had already concluded that he could not pull it off. Bert Fields had delivered the bad news. True, under California’s seven-year statute, Jackson could break his CBS contract. But he still owed CBS four albums. Whoever signed him could be sued for the estimated combined earnings of those albums—a sum greater than the gross national product of Uganda.

Geffen had to settle for a consolation prize. Jackson owned two publishing companies, the larger of which, ATV Music, held most of the Beatles’ copyrights. Suddenly, ATV dropped EMI Music as its administrator and hired MCA Music. It was easier to see how this benefited MCA Corporation employee David Geffen than it did Michael Jackson. Unlike EMI, MCA was not equipped to manage the catalog outside the United States. Therefore, as Spy magazine observed, MCA “will be obliged to hire another company—EMI, say—to perform that task.”

Though he did not get Michael Jackson on his label after all, Geffen would nevertheless have his revenge against Yetnikoff. All it would take in the end was an article by Laura Landro that appeared in the August 17, 1990, edition of The Wall Street Journal. She reported that Yetnikoff’s relations with Jackson and Springsteen had soured, and that he had just signed a new contract under which he would gradually phase out his managerial duties and groom a successor. Tommy Mottola was described as the “most logical candidate” to take his place. The article instantly turned Yetnikoff into a lame duck. In fact, he had signed a new contract, but so had Mottola and his management team. All that distinguished Walter’s agreement was that he had opted for a three-year deal, while Tommy and the others signed on for five years. Someone had leaked this information with a decidedly negative spin. Landro would not reveal her sources, but Walter was sure that Geffen and Mottola were involved.

Walter made a desperate attempt at damage control. On August 20, Sony president Norio Ohga issued a statement of support. “We could not be more pleased with CBS Records’ performance or with Walter,” he wrote. The following day, Yetnikoff promised a reporter that “events will prove I’m hardly a lame duck,” and went on to point an accusing finger at Geffen. “Are there people who want to bring me down? Probably.… I know what’s been signed, and I know who’s lost the battle in the streets, in terms of artist signing, and so do you. And you know who always comes sort of head to head with us. And pretty much consistently loses, and we know why he’s angry. And we know he’s spiritually bankrupt—don’t we? Don’t we? And we know he is upset when I call him a cocksucker. Don’t we? Is that so terrible? If he called me a pussy lapper, I would not get upset. I would say, I hope I’m as good as Jon Peters. If he thinks I’m gonna stop calling him a cocksucker, he’s wrong.”

On Friday, August 24, Billboard published Yetnikoff’s last interview as CBS Records president—in which he insisted that he would long continue as CBS Records president. In a sidebar to the article, the magazine printed a brief statement from Bruce Springsteen’s manager, Jon Landau, that probably drove the final nail into Walter’s coffin. Clearly, Landau had written his broadside in consultation with Springsteen, and it was widely assumed that he had reviewed it with the man he called his “rabbi”—David Geffen. It read, in part:

Walter Yetnikoff was a good friend to Bruce Springsteen and me for many, many years. We enjoyed a superb professional relationship and a pleasant social one. For reasons that remain obscure to us, that relationship ended not long after CBS was purchased by Sony. Neither Bruce nor I have had a significant conversation with him in nearly two years.



The same day the Billboard item appeared, Norio Ohga cut short a vacation to fly to New York for a meeting with the record boss. The myth of Walter Yetnikoff—the master schmoozer, schmingler, and bingler—had at last been shattered. The two men had a long, intense conversation. Walter was put on notice. The vendettas, the crude behavior, the geshreiing—it all had to stop, he was told. Immediately.

Ohga’s warning to Yetnikoff was most likely just a matter of procedure. “He obviously already planned to force out Walter,” said one insider. “What was he telling him? That he had to clean up his act, and conduct himself like a professional, and stop being so vitriolic? Then he was telling Walter to stop being Walter—giving him advice he couldn’t follow in a million years. It was merely a pro forma thing.”

On September 4, 1990—the very same day the indictment against Joe Isgro was tossed out—Sony announced that Walter Yetnikoff was leaving his post. After fifteen years as head of CBS Records, Yetnikoff’s reign as “king of the grooves” was over. It had fallen apart with astonishing swiftness. In one year of sobriety, Walter had turned on, or been turned on by, many of his closest friends in the business: Geffen, Grubman, Guber, Landau, Mottola, and Schulhof.

As usual, David Geffen was among the first to learn the news. He reportedly telephoned Irving Azoff, with whom he now maintained a truce. “Ding dong the witch is dead,” Geffen said. In an interview some time later, Geffen delivered an accurate one-line summation of Yetnikoff’s demise: “Walter slit his own throat.”

Mottola did not get Walter’s job—it went, for the time being, to Mickey Schulhof—even though he had made a strong case for the position. He and his team had done a surprisingly good job of restoring some of CBS’s lost market share. Two acts inherited by Mottola from Al Teller’s administration, New Kids on the Block and Michael Bolton, had broken big. Mottola had also signed and supervised Mariah Carey, one of the year’s most successful new acts. But there were still too many negatives about Tommy. No one seemed to regard him as much of an administrator, and he was unpopular with the rank and file. His style, it was said, was management by putdown.

Walter tried to be philosophical about Mottola. “Tommy has to look at himself in the mirror,” he pointed out to one friend. Walter could also take comfort in his severance pay—about $25 million—which doubled his estimated net worth. Before long, he was working out of an East Side townhouse and had created a new company called Velvel Musical Industries (“Velvel” is Yiddish for “little Walter”). Friends said he appeared happy.

On January 1, 1991, CBS Records changed its name to Sony Music Entertainment. It was particularly fitting. The company no longer bore any resemblance to the once-venerable home of Goddard Lieberson. Its past market dominance was considered unreclaimable. With all of the upheaval it had undergone, what Sony Music Entertainment needed most was a period of calm and stability. Given the nature of the music business, however, this was probably too much to hope for.


* As this edition went to press, the suit was still pending.

* Geffen recently acknowledged, in an interview in Vanity Fair, that “I date men and I date women.”





DEBTS AND SOURCES


I can’t say this book wouldn’t exist without Ruth Fecych, but it would not have been the same book. She has been a great friend and collaborator—editor is too weak a term to describe her contribution. Though I take full responsibility for its contents, I feel this book is hers as much as mine.

My deepest gratitude to two people who have since left Times Books: Elisabeth Scharlatt and Jonathan Segal. Many thanks to Beth Pearson, production editor, and Joan Marlow, copy editor, for a great job. Thanks also to Marge Anderson, Sandee Brawarsky, Kent Holland, Susan Luke, Lesley Oelsner, Linda Rosenberg, Delia Smith, Jenny Vandeventer, and Steve Wasserman.

I’m extremely grateful to all my sources, but one bears special mention. Less than a month after I interviewed Michael Hutson of Gaia Records, he died in a drowning accident. Michael was one of the most charming and straightforward people I met in the record business. His death is a terrible loss.

The greatest side benefit of working on this book was that through it I met a number of fine journalists, many of whom have become personal friends. Susan Adams, Fred Goodman, Bruce Haring, John Lombardi, Chris Mills, Jeff Ressner, Jeff Rutledge, and Adam White all gave me valuable assistance. Special thanks, as well, to Michael Gillard of the London Observer and Bill Bastone of The Village Voice.

Other journalists, some friends as well, provided assistance: David Breskin, John Campbell, Jim Carnegie, Michael Caruso, Pat Clawson, John Cooney, Hal Davis, Claudia Deutsch, Barbara Donnelly, Steve Dupler, Gene Ely, Stephen Fried, Michael Goldberg, Jill Jonnes, Neal Koch, Janet Lewis, Andrew Marton, Libby Mosier, Michael Orey, Ginnie Sidaris, Marty Spaninger, Stu Stogel, and Ken Terry. Finally, I must single out William Knoedelseder of the Los Angeles Times, who came to my aid even though he was writing a book of his own—truly a statesmanlike act. Bill’s articles, many of which are cited in the source notes, have added immeasurably to the public record on the music business.

I received help from numerous law enforcement people, including Andy Baxter, Douglas Grover, Andy Kurins, Ed McDonald, Fred Rayano, Brian Waldron, and many others I cannot name. None of these sources, I might add, ever overstepped the bounds of propriety in discussing a criminal matter.

Harry Gossett of Rowan Associates in Alexandria, Virginia, is a superb private investigator and, I’m happy to say, a friend. Harry helped me obtain declassified police reports and other documents, and generously refused to accept payment aside from expenses. Thanks, also, to Barbara Rowan.

A few people provided paid assistance. John Rosenberg of On-Line Resources in Arlington, Virginia, ran a number of computer searches for me and turned up valuable news items. Andy Kivel of Data Center in Oakland, California, found other useful clips. Two Los Angeles-based reporters, Jeff Gottlieb and Geanne Perlman, obtained court documents for me. Thanks also to Geanne for covering Sal Pisello’s arraignment in my absence.

Many thanks to the following PR and investor relations people: Dick Auletta, Sue Binford, Andrew Freedman, Richard Gersh, Gershon Kekst, Dan Klores, Barry Kluger, Ann Morfogen, Diane Millett, Jerri Perkovich, Doris Robin, Sue Satriano, Gerald Vukas, and Susan Zampolino.

I am grateful to those people who helped me obtain or gain access to useful books, articles, recordings, and documents: Mikie Harris, Lee Hasin, Bob Hyde, Carol Hyun, Mary Kate Leming, Marty London, Tina McCarthy, Richard Perry, Sarah Prown, Marian Russell, William Thomas, Chris Washington, and David Wheeler.

For hospitality and other assistance, thanks to: Bob Braunschweig, David Cudaback, Chel Dong, Steve Futterman, Peter Landau, Carole and Harry Schachter, Rita Stollman, and Gail and Diva Zappa.

Most of my library research was conducted at the Elmer Holmes Bobst Library of New York University, my alma mater. My thanks to all the helpful librarians there, and at the Lincoln Center Library for the Performing Arts.

This book was written in XyWrite III-Plus. I’m grateful to XYQUEST Inc. for creating this excellent word-processing software.

I want to single out two close friends who were instrumental in the life of this project. Fiammetta Rocco introduced me to Ruth Fecych and provided valuable advice on the subject of book writing. Edward Baker gave me the idea to do this book.

Very special thanks to L.B.S.

Last on my list but foremost in my appreciation, I would like to thank my parents, whose encouragement and support made this book possible.

1. Depiction of the Wall tour: Fink Floyd (New York: Delilah/Putnam, 1980); “Pink Floyd to Play Two Cities,” Rolling Stone, Jan. 24, 1980; “Talent in Action,” Billboard, Feb. 23, 1980; “Pink Floyd: The Wall Tour,” Rolling Stone, June 4, 1987.

2. “This is very tough stuff”: “Pink Floyd: Up Against The Wall,’ ” Rolling Stone, Feb. 7, 1980.

3. Asher’s thoughts and quotes: Als with Asher, Oct. 22, 1985; Jan. 6, 1986; June 3, 1987; June 29, 1987; Sept. 29, 1987.

4. CBS Records outspent all others: AI with Asher, Jan. 6, 1986. Also confirmed by CBS Records for the year 1985 in Joseph Isgro v. Recording Industry Association of America, Case No. 2740 (1986), Central District of California.

5. Backe’s thoughts on Asher and Yetnikoff: AI with Backe, Aug. 11, 1987.

6. The Network: Billboard first reported the term in “WCI Cos. Drop Indie Promo Reps,” Nov. 8, 1980, and named some of its key members in “MCA Studies Its Independent Promoters’ Expenditures,” Nov. 15, 1980. The term received wider currency in “Unease over Clout of the Promoters,” Los Angeles Times, Oct. 21, 1983.

7. Golf shirt story: AI with RCA executive vice-president Richard Dobbis, Apr. 30, 1987.

8. Subcontracting arrangement: Joe Isgro testified to this effect in his deposition of Apr. 22, 1987, in Isgro v. RIAA. He said he had been given projects to work by Jerry Meyers and Jerry Brenner that they in turn had got from artist managers.

9. DiSipio’s ninety stations: “The New Payola,” NBC Nightly News, June 10, 1987.

10. DiSipio and Isgro territories: DiSipio stations reported in “A Few Promoters Dominate Record Business,” The Wall Street Journal, Apr. 18, 1986. Isgro stations alleged to have accepted payola from his top employees, Ralph Tashjian and William Craig, in United States v. Ralph Tashjian, Case No. 124(B) (1988), U.S. District Court, Central District of California; and United States v. William Craig, Case No. 125 (1988), U.S. District Court, Central District of California.

11. Descriptions of Isgro and DiSipio: See Chapter Ten: Joey and Freddy.

12. “Freddy could open the shul”: AI with NBC Nightly News producer Ira Silverman, June 22, 1987.

13. Payola as the “price” of airplay: Professor Ronald Coase of the University of Chicago Law School has argued, correctly, that payola is another name for advertising. Radio play is the main means to advertise a new record. This advertising is rightly free. Coase, however, believes it should not be free and that payola should be legalized. This is absurd. Radio already receives a payment in the free use of the records. Without music, pop radio would have no listeners—and no revenues. One could as easily argue that radio should pay the labels for music. Indeed, MTV, the music video channel, pays record companies for the use of film clips.

But perhaps the professor should not be blamed for his flawed conclusion. In a Sept. 16, 1986, letter to Stanford Law’s J. Gregory Sidak, Coase complained that “one problem in dealing with this industry is that it is very difficult to get reliable information.… It soon became apparent that much of what I was told consisted of lies.”

14. Law journal study: “The ‘New Payola’ and the American Record Industry: Transactions Costs and Precautionary Ignorance in Contracts for Illicit Services,” Harvard Journal of Law & Public Policy, Summer 1987.

15. Confessions of California PD: “The New Payola,” NBC Nightly News, July 10, 1987. See also: “Ross Plans New Payola Probe,” Radio & Records, June 26, 1987.

16. Methods of paying bribes:

17. Cassette boxes and record sleeves: “Inside the Payola Scandal,” Rolling Stone, Jan. 14, 1988.

18. “There are programmers”: AI with Fred Haayen, June 18, 1987.

19. The cost of using indies: In Isgro v. RIAA, CBS, Warner Bros., MCA, and A&M disclosed their indie expenditures for 1985. The combined total for just these four companies was $30 million. It’s hard to imagine that the entire industry could have spent less than twice that amount. The $60 million estimate was cited to the author in a number of interviews, including one conducted on Oct. 23, 1987, with Jack Craigo, former president of Chrysalis Records. Others thought a more likely figure was $80 million.

20. “There was … a suspicion”: Quoted in “Payola: The Record-Label Connection,” Rolling Stone, Apr. 21, 1988.

21. “ ‘Jesse’ is legendary”: AI with RCA’s Rick Dobbis, Apr. 30, 1987.

22. “it wasn’t payola”: AI with Asher, Apr. 17, 1986.

23. “You got the feeling”: AI with Goldman, July 21, 1986.

24. “I call them claim jumpers”: AI with Gallis, June 27, 1987.

25. “The line about Freddy”: AI with Danny Davis, Feb. 29, 1988. He was referring to when he was vice-president of pop promotion at Motown in the late seventies.

26. The Network contract and R&R: A perfect illustration of the Network-R&R symbiosis could be found in the contract given Joe Isgro by MCA Records on January 1, 1986, for his promotion services. Under the heading compensation, the contract read, in part, “You will be eligible to receive a bonus payment when your promotion and exploitation activities for a Recording in the Territory result in that Recording being broadcast on so-called ‘P1,’ ‘P2’ and ‘P3’ … class radio stations in the Territory.… Your bonus payment … shall be $2,500 for each P1 station, $750 for each P2 station and $500 for each P3 station … unless you and we have mutually agreed upon a greater or lesser amount [emphasis added].” (A promotion contract given to Isgro by Warner Bros. Records on July 1, 1983, had identical wording, except the bonus for a P1 was $1,500. Of course, this was three years earlier.)

R&R had created and was the sole user of the designations P1, P2, and P3 (the “P” stands for “parallel”). These were specific stations ranked by descending size order—P1s had the most listeners—that R&R judged to be leaders in their respective regions. R&R’s charts were calculated solely from the playlists of these stations. As Isgro’s contract makes clear, the Network promoter was being paid, above all else, to get a record a good chart position in R&R.

27. Paper adds: Als with Bob Wilson, July 28, 1987 and Mar. 15, 1988. Also explained in detail to author on same date by Danny Davis, ex-employee of Isgro Enterprises. See also: “Record Firms Don’t Always Get the Radio Play They Pay For,” Los Angeles Times, Mar. 19, 1984.

50. Common justifications for payola: This is based on numerous interviews by the author.

Yetnikoff himself used the lobbying analogy in a 1986 interview with John Lombardi (the exact date is not in Lombardi’s notes; probably March or April). Speaking of the indies, Yetnikoff said, “Why do people use lobbyists in Washington? In the disc business, they call it payola; in Washington, they call it lobbying.”

52. “They get results”: “King of the Schmooze.”





3 Lullaby of Gangland


1. Lubinsky’s failure to pay royalties: AI with former Savoy A&R man Lee Magid, Mar. 29, 1990. Magid said, “Nobody ever got what they should have got” from Lubinsky. Even Fred Mendelsohn, Lubinsky’s successor as Savoy president and a diehard apologist for him, had to admit that his late boss rarely paid royalties. AI with Mendelsohn Mar. 27, 1990. Mendelsohn said that artists were charged for all the costs of recording, which more than wiped out their share of the proceeds. Of course, today’s standard contracts are not much better than Savoy’s (see pp. 143–44).

2. “We were all characters”: This and all subsequent Levy quotes, unless noted, from AIs on Feb. 24, 1987; Apr. 29, 1987; May 27, 1987; and July 30, 1988.

3. “Octopus” and “Godfather”: On Oct. 30, 1957, Variety ran an item entitled “Big Wheel and New Disk Deals (Morris Levy, Music Octopus).” It was a reprint of observations made the previous week by noted jazz critic Ralph Gleason in his column for the San Francisco Chronicle. Gleason described Levy as a “lean, muscular New Yorker who … heads a music empire of staggering proportions.” In an interview on Apr. 29, 1987, Morris complained about the “Octopus” moniker. “That didn’t help me, as time went by,” he said. NBC-TV’s Brian Ross used the “Godfather” handle in an interview with Levy on Today, NBC News, Sept. 24, 1986.

4. “It’s always pennies”: “Frankie Lymon: A Lover’s Legacy,” West 57th, CBS News, July 9, 1986.

5. Levy’s personal finances:

6. $75 million net worth: Author’s estimate, not disputed by Levy.

7. Number of Big Seven copyrights: “Morris Levy: Big Clout in Record Industry: His Behind-the-Scenes Influence Is Felt Throughout the Industry,” Los Angeles Times, July 20, 1986.

8. Sunnyview’s value: Stated by Levy for purpose of securing bail in United States v. Morris Levy, Dominick Canterino, and Howard Fisher, Case No. 301 (1986), Camden, New Jersey.

9. Strawberries bid: AI with Levy’s eldest son, Adam, Apr. 8, 1988.

10. “That don’t make me a Catholic”: Relayed to author by attorney Freddie Gershon, Mar. 16, 1988.

11. Irving Levy’s death: This is a classic example of how legend becomes fact. At least a half-dozen different sources assured the author that Irving was bumped off by the mob, and that Morris was the real target. This false assertion also turns up in an exhaustive but hardly infallible FBI document concerning Levy: “Affidavit in Support of Orders Authorizing the Interception of Wire Communications,” Lawrence Ferreira, Special Agent, Federal Bureau of Investigation, Aug. 1985; United States v. Biaggi and Esposito, Case No. 151 (1987), U.S. District Court, Eastern District of New York. The rumor appears in even more distorted form in Marc Eliot’s Rockonomics (New York: Franklin Watts, 1989), which states on p. 48 that Irving was “gunned down” by “purported gangland rivals who mistook him for Morris.”

In fact, the killer, Lee Schlesinger, was convicted of manslaughter and went to jail for the murder. Schlesinger had arrived at Birdland on Jan. 31, 1959, in the early morning hours, with his wife, Betty, a prostitute. Irving ordered her from the club, and a scuffle ensued between him and Schlesinger. The two men carried the fight outside; presently, Irving staggered back into the club, mortally wounded with a knife. No one actually witnessed the stabbing. Schlesinger’s lawyer, Herbert Lippman, told the author on Mar. 23, 1990, that his client was “one of the boys. He worked for the mob [as] a collector for shylocks.” But, Lippman added, this was a personal dispute, not a mob killing. And, clearly, Morris was not the target.

See also: “Couple Is Refused Bail in Slaying at Birdland,” (New York) Herald Tribune, Feb. 1, 1959.

12. Yetnikoff and Levy:

13. Yetnikoff’s investment in Malinowski: Confirmed by Yetnikoff in “King of the Schmooze”; and by Yetnikoff’s attorney, Stanley Schlesinger, AI, Sept. 2, 1987.

14. Investment in Levy horses by Joel, Hall, Frank Weber (Joel’s manager), and Tommy Mottola (Hall’s manager): AIs with Levy, May 27, 1987; and Adam Levy, Apr. 8, 1988.

15. “Psychiatric consultation” check: Shown to author by Adam Levy on above date.

16. Levy and the mob:

17. New York police information: Confidential source.

18. Description of Carbo: Virgil W. Peterson, The Mob: 200 Years of Organized Crime in New York (Illinois: Green Hill Publishers, 1983), pp. 328–29.

19. Greenwich Village Inn: AI with Levy, July 30, 1988.

20. “This kid could tear”: Quoted in “ ‘Corky’ Vastola: Music and the Mob,” The (Bergen) Record, Sept. 30, 1986.

21. Genovese history: Peterson, op. cit., pp. 316-18, 395; and Peter Maas, The Valachi Papers (New York: G.P. Putnam’s Sons, 1968), pp. 242–45.

22. Eboli’s Promo deal: Confidential source.

23. FBI warning about Gigante: Levy on NBC’s Today, Sept. 24, 1986 (Levy did not name Gigante, but the inference was obvious).

24. Gigante’s insanity gambit: “Strange Old Man on Sullivan Street: A New Mob Power,” The New York Times, Feb. 3, 1988.

25. The brownstone: “Runnin’ Scared,” The Village Voice, Dec. 8, 1987. The Voice reporter, William Bastone, calculated the $16,000 sale price from the transfer tax on the property deed. Levy insisted to the author that he had sold the brownstone to Esposito for exactly what he had paid for it. But the author examined the same deed transfer documents—for 67 East Seventy-seventh Street, Block 01392, Lot 0129, New York County—and reached the same conclusion as Bastone. The clear implication is that Levy paid tribute to Gigante, or laundered money for him, via Esposito. For more evidence of this, see the Ferreira affidavit and “The Godfather of Rock & Roll,” Rolling Stone, Nov. 17, 1988.

26. Goldner information: “Frankie Lymon, the Teenagers: A Recording History,” booklet published by Murray Hill Records & Tapes for five-record Lymon reissue, 1986. For an amusing account of how Goldner formed Red Bird with Leiber and Stoller, see: Ted Fox, In the Groove: The People Behind the Music (New York: St. Martin’s Press, 1986), pp. 182–83.

27. Alan Freed history: Ed Ward, Geoffrey Stokes, and Ken Tucker, Rock of Ages: The Rolling Stone History of Rock and Roll (New York: Rolling Stone Press/Summit Books, 1986), pp. 69–70, 96; and Steve Chapple and Reebee Garofalo, Rock ‘n’ Roll Is Here to Pay: The History and Politics of the Music Industry (Chicago: Nelson Hall, 1977), pp. 56–57.

28. “Anybody who says”: Ibid., p. 56.

29. J. Moriarty story: Confirmed in AIs with Gayles, June 1, 1987; and Hooke, Mar. 24, 1988. Both these men told the story a bit differently than Levy, in each case making himself the principal foil.

30. Lew Piatt information: Confirmed by Freed authority Jeff Rutledge in July 20, 1989, letter to author.

31. Earl Wilson interview: “Alan Freed Telling All: Meets Payola Probers,” New York Post, Nov. 25, 1959. The type size is indeed enormous.

32. Weakness of payola statute: See also: “$1 Million in Suspected ‘New Payola’ is Probed,” Los Angeles Times, Dec. 22, 1987.

33. Congressional hearings: Payola and Other Deceptive Practices in the Broadcasting Field. Hearings before a Subcommittee of the Committee on Interstate and Foreign Commerce, House of Representatives, Eighty-sixth Congress, second session. See also: “The Entertainer: After Three Decades on TV, Dick Clark Is Busier than Ever,” The Wall Street Journal, Mar. 25, 1985.

34. Elektra’s $27 million loss: “A Wildcatter in the Record Biz,” Manhattan, inc., May 1989.

35. “the man who is to the record industry”: AI with Smith, July 21, 1987.

36. “Morris gave me back the demo”: AI with Cordell, Mar. 21, 1988.

37. “He’s entitled”: AI with Weiss, Mar. 23, 1988.

38. Lennon case: Big Seven Music Corp. v. John Lennon, Case No. 2924 (1975), Supreme Court of the State of New York, County of New York. Appellate court opinion: 409 F. Supp. 122 (1976).

39. “You get together”: Deposition of Morris Levy, Apr. 18, 1985; Emira Lymon v. Morris Levy, U.S. District Court, Southern District of New York.

40. “If you screw him”: AI with Art Kass, Jan. 14, 1988.

41. “Given where we came from”: AI with Weiss, Mar. 23, 1988.

42. Jimmy Weston’s incident: “Gigante Asks Probe of D.A. Office in Assault on Cop,” (New York) Daily News, July 2, 1975.

43. “Morris told me, Louie”: AI with Gigante, Apr. 6, 1988.

44. McCalla information:

45. Date and details of murder; height and weight: Fort Lauderdale, Florida, Police Department offense report, Case No. 15317 (1980); and Certificate of Death for Nathan Calvin McCalla, State of Florida, File No. 204, Feb. 20, 1980.

46. Military background; JAMF name; “If I was going to describe”: AI with Barry Fredericks, Nov. 12, 1987.

47. “Nate had a medieval mace”: AI with David Nives, Nov. 17, 1987.

48. Washington police information: AIs with D.C. policemen Carl Shoffler, Feb. 29, 1988, and Joe Quantrille, Mar. 16, 1988. Also: Washington, D.C., police report, June 8, 1983.

UJA dinner: All details and dialogue from Sliced Steak, a 1973 movie documentary of the dinner, by Richard Perry.

49. Levy requested Smith as emcee: AI with Smith, July 21, 1987.

50. “I was the payola king”: AI with Weiss, Mar. 23, 1988.

51. Extortion case:

52. Heroin allegations: Oct. 28, 1988, sentencing of Levy in U.S. v. Levy.

53. Testimonial letters: AI with Levy, July 30, 1988.

54. Details of cutout deal: Testimony in U.S. v. Levy.

55. LaMonte’s criminal record: Criminal docket of John Donald LaMonte, Docket No. 438 (1977), United States District Court, Philadelphia.

56. FBI wiretaps: Government’s exhibits 104, 107, 116, and 138 in U.S. v. Levy.

57. Vastola and music:

58. Vastola and Freed: AI with Freed acquaintance Bob Rolontz, Mar. 20, 1988; confirmed to author by Gayles, June 9, 1987.

59. Vastola and Lymon: Deposition of Morris Levy, Apr. 18, 1985, Lymon v. Levy.

60. Vastola and Cleftones: AI with Levy, July 30, 1988.

61. Vastola and Queens Booking: AI with then-U.S. Attorney for Newark, Thomas Greelish, Jan. 26, 1987; confirmed by Levy in Lymon deposition.

62. Vastola and Davis horse farm: Davis’s testimony in Organized Crime in Sports (Racing). Hearings before the Select Committee on Crime, House of Representatives, Ninety-second Congress, second session.





4 Goddard and Clive


1. Lieberson information:

2. personal history; Cronkite quote; bons mots (unless otherwise noted): Goddard Lieberson commemorative album, private issue, Columbia Records, 1977; and “Goddard Lieberson, Who Fostered LPs at Columbia Records, Dies,” The New York Times, May 30, 1977.

3. Stool sample: AI with Bruce Lundvall, May 18, 1987.

4. Signed his letters “God”: AI with Arthur Taylor, Apr. 28, 1987.

5. “He spent the whole meeting”: AI with John Eastman, Dec. 17, 1987.

6. Paley and Stanton: Metz, op. cit., pp. 243–244; and Halberstam, op. cit., pp. 153–154.

7. Shepard and Lieberson: “Capturing Broadway on Record,” The New York Times Magazine, July 15, 1984.

8. My Fair Lady investment: Metz, op. cit., p. 400.

9. “Big Red” and “Mediocre Orange”: AI with Ron Alexenburg, Feb. 10, 1987.

10. Columbia history: Roland Gelatt, The Fabulous Phonograph: Eighteen Seventy-Seven to Nineteen Seventy-Seven (New York: Macmillan, 1977).

11. Lieberson and the LP: “Goddard Lieberson, Who Fostered LPs at Columbia Records, Dies.” For an amusing account of RCA’s discomfiture over Goldmark’s invention of the LP, see: Peter C. Goldmark and Lee Edson, Maverick Inventor: My Turbulent Years at CBS (New York: Saturday Review Press/E. P. Dutton, 1973), pp. 141–44.

12. Mitch Miller information: Fox, op. cit., pp. 25–71. Miller denies in his interview with Fox that he loathed rock, but this is flatly contradicted by his own words, quoted by Representative Derounian at the 1960 Congressional hearings on payola: “You would not invite these unwashed kids [rock and roll musicians] into your living room to meet your family. Why thrust them into the living rooms of your audience?”

13. John Hammond information: John Hammond and Irving Townsend, John Hammond on Record (New York: Summit Books, 1977), pp. 9–11, 59–60, 95–104, 351–53.

14. “I would go to A&R”: AI with Lundvall, Jan. 26, 1987.

15. Distribution system history: AI with Jack Craigo, Oct. 23, 1987, and Bill Gallagher, Mar. 28, 1990.

16. “the Pope”; “confessions”: AI with Gallagher protégé Tom Noonan, July 27, 1987.

17. Ertegun and Wexler: “Eclectic, Reminiscent, Amused, Fickle, Perverse,” (Profile of Ahmet Ertegun), The New Yorker, May 29, 1978 (Part I), and June 5, 1978 (Part II).

18. Ertegun as “character”: Ertegun has consistently got adoring press, the New Yorker profile being one prime example. Another is Dorothy Wade and Justine Picardie’s Music Man: Ahmet Ertegun, Atlantic Records, and the Triumph of Rock ‘n’ Roll (New York: W. W. Norton, 1990). According to legend, Ertegun’s Atlantic paid royalties to black performers when other labels did not. In the otherwise cynical Rock ‘n’ Roll Is Here to Pay, Ertegun tells a story of how another record executive in the fifties admonished him: “ ‘You’re paying those people royalties? You must be out of your mind!’ Of course, he didn’t call them ‘people.’ He called them something else.”

The fact is, Atlantic’s royalty record may have been better than most, but it was hardly impeccable. The reader is advised to take a look at “Atlantic Records Refigures Royalty Status for 35 of Its Vet R&B Artists,” Variety, May 18, 1988. In it, Sam Moore of Sam & Dave and Ruth Brown both complain of not having received a royalty check in over two decades, despite numerous reissue albums—and they are not isolated examples.

Even Lee Magid, the former A&R man for the notorious Herman Lubinsky, complained about Ertegun. He told the author on Mar. 29, 1990, that the Clovers’ version of a song he wrote, “I Played the Fool,” was recently reissued on Atlantic—with Ahmet Ertegun listed as the author. Magid said he pointed this out to Ertegun, who apologized for the “mistake.”

19. “he went legit”: AI with Levy, July 30, 1988.

20. Clive’s early history and rise to Columbia president: Clive Davis and James Willwerth, Clive: Inside the Record Business (New York: William Morrow, 1975), pp. 7–29. In an AI on Mar. 28, 1990, Bill Gallagher declined to discuss the details of his power struggle with Clive. He said only, “My departure just had to be. It was either him or me.”

21. “I had no A&R training”: This and all subsequent Davis quotes in this chapter, unless otherwise noted, are from his autobiography.

Clive and Teller: AI with Teller, Apr. 13, 1987.

22. “That ego”: AI with Craigo, Oct. 23, 1987.

23. “Let me read”: AI with Smith, July 21, 1987.

24. Clive and the CD: AI with Rose Gross-Marino, Clive’s administrative assistant in the eighties, Feb. 25, 1987.

25. Clive, Hammond, and Dylan: Hammond and Townsend, op. cit., p. 355–56; and Davis and Willwerth, op. cit., pp. 49–50. It’s interesting to note that the two accounts differ somewhat. Clive wrote that he persuaded Hammond to appeal to Dylan as a friend, and that the singer voluntarily rescinded his letter. I’ve gone with Hammond’s account, in which Clive more or less forced Dylan to remain on Columbia.

26. Clive and Goddard’s wardrobe: AI with Davis, Apr. 30, 1987. Clive waxed rhapsodic about how Goddard’s handkerchiefs matched his shirts.

27. “needed a buffer”; “the original job”; “Goddard would never”: Ibid.

28. Clive hires Yetnikoff:

29. “rough around the edges”; “I wouldn’t say Lieberson”: Ibid.

30. Yetnikoff quotes: Joe Smith and Mitchell Fink, Off the Record: An Oral History of Popular Music (New York: Warner Books, 1988), p. 334; and Goodman’s interview with Yetnikoff, May 26, 1988.

31. Clive hires Asher: AI with Asher, Jan. 9, 1987.

32. “It’s hard to overestimate”: AI with Asher, Apr. 21, 1987.

33. Clive, Monterey, Joplin: Davis and Willwerth, op. cit., pp. 74–90; Smith and Fink, op. cit., pp. 257–58 (“the creative turning point”; “it was clear”); Ward, Stokes, and Tucker, op. cit., pp. 373–76.

34. “Clive came back to New York”: AI with Asher, Jan. 9, 1987.

35. “I hope you didn’t fuck us”: Quoted in “From Ashes to Asher: PolyGram Prexy Leads Way to the Top,” Hits, June 29, 1987.

36. “everybody ran to the index”: AI with Yetnikoff, Mar. 30, 1987.

37. “If there’s fallout”: AI with Davis, Apr. 30, 1987.

38. Clive on Schein’s parsimony: Davis and Willwerth, op. cit., p. 57.

39. “I bought some suede boots”: Lombardi notes.

40. “He was tongue-tied”: AI with Lundvall, Jan. 26, 1987.

46. “There’s a story”: AI with Yetnikoff, Mar. 30, 1987.

47. “I thought Clive was terrific”: AI with Asher, Apr. 21, 1987.

5 Newark


1. Announcement of Clive’s firing:

2. Alexenburg’s recollections: AIs with Alexenburg, July 7, 1986, and Feb. 10, 1987.

3. “A funny thing happened”: AIs with Asher, Apr. 21, 1987; and Craigo, Oct. 23, 1987. Both men remembered the line exactly the same way.

4. Clive feels unappreciated: Davis and Willwerth, op. cit., p. 280. This book is the source of all Davis quotes in this chapter, unless otherwise noted.

An interesting fact published here for the first time is that over the Memorial Day weekend, just before Clive was fired, his attorney, Eric Kronfeld, was in London, wrapping up a secret deal with L. G. Wood, the chairman of EMI London. EMI was going to hire Clive from CBS and give him his own new label. Then Clive was fired, and a short time later, Wood wrote Kronfeld a letter terminating the deal. In it, Wood refused even to mention Clive Davis by name, saying only that any meeting of “the parties” would be “inappropriate.”

5. Expense-account charges:

6. CBS allegations: Columbia Broadcasting System v. Clive Davis, Case No. 9634 (1973), Supreme Court of New York.

7. “CBS is idiotic”: Quoted in “Clive Davis Ousted; Payola Coverup Charged,” Rolling Stone, July 5, 1973.

8. Clive and Gamble and Huff:

9. Gamble and Huff history: Ward, Stokes, and Tucker, op. cit., pp. 501–2; “The Day the Soul Train Crashed,” Philadelphia, June 1983.

6 Walter’s War


1. “creative wars”: Davis and Willwerth, op. cit, p. 279.

8. Goddard and Irwin:

9. “There was a little too much”: Quoted in Metz, op. cit., p. 386.

10. “I could walk into his office”: AI with Segelstein, Feb. 5, 1987.

57. 128. “sort of stupid”: Quoted in Smith and Fink, op. cit., p. 335.

Another example of how the war continued well into the eighties was the MTV affair of 1986–87, as described to the author by a confidential MTV insider. MTV had made deals with all the big record companies to get exclusive use of certain videos in exchange for payment. In 1986 CBS Records was the first record company up for renewal. But CBS was bashing MTV in the press over its programming choices and threatening not to renew. Mo Ostin phoned MTV president Bob Pittman with an idea. Suppose the Warner labels renewed their contracts a year early? Would that give MTV leverage in its struggle with CBS? Pittman was gleeful. Once Warner renewed, all the other majors followed—all but CBS. Now the odd man out, CBS found itself in a corner, still fighting MTV in the press but anxious for a way to renew without losing face. In 1987 CBS quietly signed a new contract with MTV.
