“Once I yelled at Allen”: Adams notes.

36. Further corroboration of Walter’s role in helping Grubman build his practice: In a Sept. 22, 1987, AI, with industry veteran Elliot Goldman, who described himself as a close friend of both Grubman and Mike Tannen, he said the following: “Walter would have no hesitation [throwing clients to Grubman]. You want to renegotiate your deal? Fine. I don’t talk to Mike Tannen. Go hire Allen Grubman and do a new deal. The end result is, [Grubman] does go in and get them good deals. If Walter doesn’t like somebody, he’s not only going to take a business position, he’s going to take a personal position. That’s what it’s all about. And I would find it very hard to do that. But I think Walter’s attitude is, Look, this is a business of people and relationships, and I’m easier to deal with when I deal with somebody I like.”

Here he was, representing big clients, making big deals. He was respected by the presidents of record companies. When you have access to record company presidents, you’re powerful. Whoever has the most access the quickest, wins.”

9 Casablanca


1. Bogart’s drug abuse: Denied by Joyce Bogart in AI, July 22, 1987. Confirmed by Danny Davis in AI, July 21, 1987. In an interview with Russ Regan on July 23, 1987, I asked whether he believed drugs hastened Bogart’s death. He said, “I can’t speculate on that. The man was charismatic, truly a genius. But all I know is, if you put enough chemicals in your body, it’s gonna kill you. I don’t care who you are.” In an AI on Mar. 26, 1990, David Shein, who worked with Bogart at Casablanca and Boardwalk, noted, “I don’t think there’s anybody in this industry who didn’t do drugs at some time. Let’s not bullshit each other. Neil was no different. Who the hell didn’t in those days?”

One other article bears special mention: “The General Motors of the Record Business,” Washington Post, May 4, 1980. It is essentially an interview with Solleveld that ends with this ludicrous assertion by the writer, Joseph Mc-Lellan: “Where Solleveld and his company will go from here is still uncertain, but the future looks a lot more secure for them than it does for some other companies that have tried to get rich quick by following passing trends.” By this time, PolyGram was hemorrhaging money. Either Solleveld was putting one over on McLellan—who did not do his homework in any case—or else the executive was blithely unaware of what was happening to his company. I suspect the latter.

43. Dileo’s arrest records: Commonwealth of Pennsylvania v. Frank M. Dileo, Case No. 7702351A, May 5, 1977; and Commonwealth of Pennsylvania v. Frank M. Dileo, Case No. 7803525A, July 20, 1978. Both times Dileo pleaded guilty to two counts: 1) poolselling and bookmaking; and 2) possession of an instrument of crime (the rice paper). The first time, his punishment was a $1,000 fine; the second time, a $300 fine. Go figure.

The first arrest form carried this descriptive paragraph: “On Mar. 28, 1977, pursuant to a search warrant for the actor and his premises, 163 North South Drive, Ross Township, Pa., the police found the actor had possession of a sheet of rice paper on which was recorded $2,305 in sports bets on the result of the college basketball game between Marquette and North Carolina colleges. Both phones on the premises rang intermittently, answered by the police officers, and the callers placed sports bets on the aforesaid game, totaling $670. Also found on the premises were three sheets of paper indicating apparent owes and 25 sheets of rice paper.”

44. Dileo failed to collect on insurance: He complained about this vociferously to my source, who did not want to be named.

13 The Firing


1. Yetnikoff before the analysts: The meeting, on Mar. 15, 1983, was depicted in: “CBS Sees Strong First Quarter,” Billboard, Mar. 26, 1983.

2. Yetnikoff and MCA offer: “Yetnikoff to Stay at CBS,” Billboard, Apr. 9, 1983.

3. Asher’s thoughts prior to firing, and quotes: AI with Asher, Sept. 29, 1987.

4. “Just before Dick arrived”: AI with Jamieson, Sept. 3, 1987.

5. Account of Asher’s firing: AI with Asher, Sept. 29, 1987.

“Dick would only consider it”: AI with Schein, Jan. 8, 1987.

6. “Dick had a very naive attitude”: AI with Braun, July 14, 1987.

7. “When he was fired”: AI with Dileo, July 16, 1987.

8. Yetnikoff and the Rolling Stones:

9. Steven Wright quip: I Have a Pony, Warner Bros. Records, 4–25335.

10. $28 million: Widely accepted as the correct figure, though never confirmed by CBS. Reported, probably not for the first time, in: “Pop Records Go Boom,” New York, October 31, 1983. Confirmed in Nov. 20, 1984, AI with lawyer Eric Kronfeld. Kronfeld said he was set to become Jagger’s attorney until Walter prevented it—if true, another example of Yetnikoff’s ability to influence who represented his company’s artists. Kronfeld also confirmed that Ertegun’s bid was half that of Walter’s.

11. “So we’re in the Ritz”: “King of the Schmooze,” Esquire, Nov. 1986.

12. Yetnikoff and Emon birthday parties: AIs with Emon, Sept. 21, 1987, and Oct. 20, 1989; Winter, July 30, 1987; and confidential guest at Emon party (on cocaine use).

13. Yetnikoff before the analysts—again: CBS Records transcript of security analysts meeting, Nov. 8, 1983.

14. “That’s unheard of”: Quoted in “Walter Yetnikoff: The Most Powerful Man in the Record Business,” Rolling Stone, Dec. 15–29, 1988.

15. “Michael Jackson would ask Walter”: AI with Schlesinger, Sept. 2, 1987.

16. Yetnikoff and Warner negotiations: AIs with Elliot Goldman, May 13, 1987, and Schlesinger, Sept. 9, 1987. Goldman, who handled the negotiations for Warner as senior vice-president, allowed me to see some internal memos he had written on the subject. In early 1984 Steve Ross asked Goldman to study the possibility of having Walter Yetnikoff replace Ahmet Ertegun as head of Atlantic. The study had a code name—the “YGP” project—to prevent leaks. YGP stood for Yetnikoff, Guber, and Peters. Apparently, Walter was offering himself to Warner as part of a package that would include Peter Guber and Jon Peters (see pp. 318–19). Goldman suggested that Walter be given a five-year contract with an annual salary of $500,000, plus a $1 million signing bonus. He was less enthused about Guber and Peters. “GP’s talents in the record industry are virtually nil in my judgment,” he wrote in a March 1984 memo to Ross. By October, Goldman informed Ross that he was more and more convinced that the YGP deal would not close, and that Walter was “simply using the process to get CBS as high as he can.” He was right.

17. Asher after the firing: AI with Asher, Sept. 29, 1987.

18. Fox, Reed, and Loshak were forced out: confirmed in AIs with Reed and Loshak, Mar. 23, 1990.

19. “Dick walked over to Dempsey”: AI with music publicist Richard Gersh, Sept. 29, 1987.

16 The Board Meeting


1. “The record industry has created”: AI with then-Bee Gees manager Dick Ashby, Sept. 25, 1985.

2. Indie promotion in 1985:

3. Ostin and Thyret opposed to indies: On Nov. 23, 1987, Danny Davis told me, “Joe [Isgro] carried on about Mo Ostin—I didn’t know he was so dead set against [indie promotion]. Although Russ Thyret loves the fact that he paid less for indies than anybody else. He was delighted when he didn’t have to use them.”

4. What Warner and CBS spent: Made public in Isgro v. RIAA. It was further disclosed that MCA spent $9 million (as stated on p. 265), and that A&M spent more than $2 million.

5. Rolling Stone estimate: “Payola: The Record-Label Connection,” Rolling Stone, Apr. 21, 1988.

6. Walter’s relationship with Fred DiSipio: “King of the Schmooze,” Esquire, Nov. 1986, quotes Yetnikoff: “I am—I’ve said it before and I’ll say it again—a friend of Freddie DiSipio’s. Fred is good at what he does; Joe Isgro is good at what he does, on the West Coast.”

7. Azoff, Geffen, and indie promotion:

8. Isgro’s boast about Quarterflash: Mar. 1986 interview with Isgro by anonymous reporter.

9. Azoff’s attack on R&R: “MCA Pulls Trade-Paper Ads in Feud,” Los Angeles Times, July 7, 1985.

10. Azoff accuses Walter: Esquire quote from “King of the Schmooze”; deposition quote made public in a pretrial brief in Isgro v. RIAA.

Circumstantial evidence that Azoff was trying to rein in Walter’s use of indies can be found in the text of a letter disclosed in a brief in Isgro v. RIAA. In the fall of 1985, Sid Sheinberg, Azoff’s boss, wrote to Tom Wyman, Walter’s then-boss: “In our judgment, the questions raised by rumors concerning the practices of independent promoters in the record industry require the attention of corporate management.”

11. “conspiracy of silence”: Quoted in “Payola Investigation Launched,” Washington Post, Apr. 3, 1986.

12. Oct. 1, 1985, RIAA meeting:

13. MCA, Motown, and Arista; text of resolution: Disclosed in various briefs in Isgro v. RIAA.

16. Pisello/MCA scandal: The best primary sources are: Government’s Sentencing Memorandum Pursuant to 18-USC-3577, United States v. Salvatore James Pisello, Case No. 818 (1984), District Court, Central District of California; and trial transcript in: United States v. Salvatore James Pisello (1987), District Court, Central District of California.

The “organized crime concept” remark made by Newark prosecutor Bruce Repetto at Oct. 28, 1988, sentencing of Morris Levy in U.S. v. Levy.

There has been an enormous body of press coverage on this subject as well, ever since William Knoedelseder of the Los Angeles Times broke the story on May 10, 1985. For further reading, I recommend:

17. From the Los Angeles Times: “MCA Audit Details Ties to Pisello,” May 10, 1985; “Probe Points to Mob Role in Record Deal,” Mar. 3, 1986; “Salvatore Pisello: A Shadowy Figure in Records Deals,” May 4, 1986; “Prosecutor Benched After Getting His Man,” Jan. 22, 1989.

During the convention, I met Ron Kyle, Matty Singer, and Helene Masiko, employees of Intercontinental and MidAtlantic. I got the company names from them. Curiously, neither company was listed in the phone book.

41. Rudnick on payola case: AI with Rudnick, May 18, 1988.

42. Ross and Dantzer: I was with Ross in Los Angeles later that day (July 9, 1987), and he told me the story. If you watch Dantzer’s lips in the broadcast, you can make out the words.

43. Davis leaves Isgro: AI with Davis, Apr. 4, 1988.

44. Martell dinner: That Azoff stiffed the Martell Foundation became inadvertently known to me during a July 31, 1987, interview with publicist Norman Winter, when Winter took a phone call from chairman Tony Martell to discuss the problem.

There is a further irony in the industry’s so honoring Azoff while excommunicating Isgro. Up until the first NBC story broke, both Isgro and DiSipio were on the Martell Foundation’s advisory board, according to pre-1986 dinner program booklets.

45. “I applaud them … I’m a gangster and every other fucking name.…”: AI with Isgro, July 29, 1987.

46. “He thought he was doing us a favor”: Quoted in “King of the Schmooze,” Esquire, Nov. 1986.

47. Collapse of MCA probe: See articles cited in notes to pp. 281–83.

49. Denouement of Isgro lawsuit:

19 The Trial


1. “The evidence I’ve got”: Quoted in “Payola: The Record-Label Connection,” Rolling Stone, Apr. 21, 1988.

2. Indictments: See notes to p. 12.

3. “heavy-handed,” etc.: “Tashjians, Craig, Wilson Indicted,” Radio & Records, Mar. 4, 1988.

4. Judgments in Tashjian and Craig cases:

5. Charges against Valerie thrown out: “Valerie Tashjian Tax Charges Dismissed,” Radio & Records, May 19, 1989.

6. Ralph’s guilty plea: “Tashjian Pleads Guilty to One Payola Count,” Radio & Records, May 26, 1989.

7. Craig’s guilty plea: “Craig Pleads Guilty to Payola Charge,” Radio & Records, Oct. 20, 1989.

8. Isgro, Moore, and 20th Century-Fox: AI with Isgro, Aug. 3, 1989; and “Isgro, Fox Plan Film on Hoffa,” Daily Variety, Dec. 29, 1989.

9. Indictments of Isgro, Anderson, and Monka: United States v. Joseph Isgro et al., Case No. 951 (1989), U.S. District Court, Central District of California.

10. Monka a drug offender: “Payola Probe’s ‘Mystery Man’ Had State Political Entree,” Daily Variety, Dec. 7, 1989.

11. Arrest of Monka and Isgro; Isgro’s confrontation with Silverman: AIs with Silverman, Dec. 13 and 15, 1989.

12. Isgro’s bail hearing: “Record Promoter Accused of Ties to Mob,” The (Santa Monica) Outlook, Dec. 6, 1989.

13. n. Smith’s incarceration: “King of Spin, or Just a Kingpin?” (Jerry Capeci’s “Gang Land” column), (New York) Daily News, Dec. 19, 1989.

14. Tashjian’s sentencing: “Tashjian ‘Turnaround’ Spurs Light Sentence,” Radio & Records, Dec. 15, 1989.

15. Craig’s sentencing: “Craig Gets Probation, 60G Fine,” Daily Variety, Dec. 19, 1989.

16. Goodman case:

17. Indictment: United States v. Howard Goodman, Case No. 20305-H (1989), U.S. District Court, Western District of Tennessee.

18. Goodman a subcontractor for a Network member: “Payola Probe Snares Goodman,” Daily Variety, Dec. 15, 1989.

19. Details of trial: “Goodman Convicted on Payola Charges,” Radio & Records, July 6, 1990; “Indie Promoter Convicted of Payola Charges,” Billboard, July 14, 1990.

20. “I would have tried”: AI with Rudnick, March 21, 1991.

21. Lynch’s background and reputation: “Lynch Mob Heads West,” Legal Times, Apr. 17, 1989.

22. DiRicco case: DiRicco was hit with two indictments on Jan. 5, 1988, in a coordinated effort by the U.S. Attorney for the Northern District of California and the San Francisco office of the Organized Crime Drug Enforcement Task Force. In one case, he was indicted with Kevin Leong, a former investigator for the Alameda County District Attorney’s Office, on charges of conspiring to distribute cocaine, launder drug money, and obstruct justice. In the other, DiRicco was the sole defendant, charged with aiding in the preparation of fraudulent tax returns. DiRicco and Leong were both found guilty by a San Francisco jury in the drug-related case. DiRicco then pleaded guilty to the tax charges, and subsequently pleaded guilty to additional tax-related charges. He was sentenced in all three cases to five years’ probation. In an AI on March 20, 1991, Geoffrey Anderson, the lead prosecuting attorney, would neither confirm nor deny that DiRicco got a break on his sentencing in return for cooperating in the Los Angeles case against Isgro.

23. Details of Isgro trial testimony: “At Payola Trial, Primer on Forms and Mechanics,” The New York Times, Aug. 25, 1990; “Judge Threatens to Void Payola Case,” The New York Times, Aug. 30, 1990; “Isgro Trial Opens, Five Payola Witnesses Testify,” Radio & Records, Aug. 31, 1990; “Isgro Trial Testimony Bares Payoffs,” Billboard, Sept. 8, 1990

24. Walker cochairman of a “Just Say No” program: “The New Payola,” NBC Nightly News, Feb. 26, 1988.

25. Ideman dismisses case and rebukes government: “Payola Case Is Dismissed by Judge,” The New York Times, Sept. 5, 1990; “Case Against Isgro Dismissed by Judge in Payola Probe,” The Wall Street Journal, Sept. 5, 1990; “Judge Pulls Plug on Isgro Payola Trial,” Billboard, Sept. 15, 1990

26. Rudnick’s exoneration: Misconduct by Senior Managers in the Internal Revenue Service, House of Representatives Report 101-800, Committee on Government Operations, John Conyers, chairman, Oct. 4, 1990. Quote from p. 67.

27. Return of Network system: Confirmed by numerous industry people, including consultant and former PolyGram head Dick Asher, in AI, Jan. 3, 1991.

28. Isgro and Vastola: In a Feb. 19, 1991, phone interview with Isgro conducted by freelance journalist Jeff Rutledge, Isgro at first denied knowing that Vastola owned City Lights. Then, after Rutledge pressed him, Isgro allowed, “Okay, yeah, maybe it’s possible. Maybe I’m so fucking dumb that I don’t know that Tommy is the real owner.… You’re probably right, he probably is the owner, and yes, I know this doesn’t look good for me.…” Ten days later, Rutledge spoke to Isgro again by phone about the matter, and Isgro patched both Vastola and Vastola’s son, Guy, into the phone conversation. Isgro and Guy both denied that there was anything improper about the business arrangement. See also: “Joe Isgro Lights Up Highway 33,” Daily Variety, Feb. 15, 1991.





20 Walter’s Fall


1. “I’ve come of age”: AI with Grubman, Feb. 20, 1991.

2. Grubman’s finder’s fee for SBK, run-in with Geffen over Chrysalis: The finder’s fee estimate was provided by a knowledgeable inside source to Bill Knoedelseder of the Los Angeles Times, and corroborated for me by SBK insider Artie Mogull in an AI on April 1, 1991. A different, confidential source provided convincing details of the Grubman/Geffen rift.

3. Purchase of Island and A&M: Prospectus dated Nov. 7, 1989, for initial public offering of thirty-two million shares of PolyGram.

4. Asher leaves PolyGram: AI with Asher, Dec. 6, 1989. Fine quotes from “P’Gram Defends Exec Exits,” Billboard, Mar. 31, 1990.

5. Geffen and Ross; sale of Geffen’s company: “The Richest Man in Hollywood,” Forbes, Dec. 24, 1990; “Hollywood’s King Cashes Out,” Vanity Fair, Feb. 1991; “David Is Goliath,” Vanity Fair, Mar. 1991; “Doesn’t David Geffen Know the Eighties Are Over?” GQ, Mar. 1991; “Who’s the Biggest Man in Hollywood?,” Spy, Apr. 1991.

6. “The entire industry is jealous”: Confidential source.

7. Sale of MCA Corporation: “The Record Man with Flawless Timing,” The New York Times, Dec. 9, 1990.

8. Yetnikoff’s troubles:

9. Marital problems: First noted in “Sony Boom,” Los Angeles Herald-Examiner, Sept. 29, 1989 (Mitchell Fink’s gossip column). After that, it became an open secret in the record business that Yetnikoff was splitting with Slamar. A Wall Street Journal article on Aug. 17, 1990, “CBS Records Chief Ready to Phase Out Managerial Duties,” described Yetnikoff as being “in the midst of a second divorce.” Fink’s item also broke the news of Yetnikoff’s sojourn at Hazelden, which he later openly admitted in interviews.

10. Alcoholism: “I was drinking a lot, enough to kill myself,” Yetnikoff is quoted saying in “Hollywood Banzai,” Vanity Fair, Feb. 1990. This, too, was no secret in the industry. “I’ve seen Walter Yetnikoff drink two bourbons for breakfast,” record executive Fred Haayen told me on May 6, 1987. As for the bar in Yetnikoff’s office, I saw it myself during my interview with Walter on Mar. 30, 1987. See also: “The Fall of Yetnikoff,” Rolling Stone, Nov. 29, 1990, which makes this assertion: “In the years before he sought treatment, Yetnikoff could be found sitting in his office after normal business hours, drinking with friends, and he was rumored to have conducted business under the influence.”

11. Yetnikoff’s claims of spiritual rebirth: “Hollywood Banzai.”

12. “Think of it this way”: Former CBS Records executive in an interview with Fred Goodman.

13. “schmoozing, schmingling, and bingling”: “Hollywood Banzai.”

14. Sony, Yetnikoff, Columbia Pictures, and Guber and Peters: All the details and quotes in this section, unless otherwise noted below, come from “Hollywood Banzai.”

15. Schulhof’s Ph.D. thesis: “Mickey Schulhof and the Globalization of Sony,” The Washington Post, Aug. 6, 1989.

“Why 8.08 percent?”: “Sony Taking on an Even More American Look,” Los Angeles Times, Oct. 16, 1989.

16. Steering committee: “Yetnikoff to Head Record and Movie Committee at Sony,” The Wall Street Journal, Oct. 13, 1989.

17. “Our understanding”: Guber quoted in “Spinning Out,” New York, Nov. 5, 1990.

18. Yetnikoff’s self-destructive behavior:

19. Yetnikoff browbeats Schulhof: Confidential source.

20. Yetnikoff and Springsteen: The rift is noted in several of the above-mentioned articles, including “The Fall of Yetnikoff” and “Spinning Out.”

21. “He took a fourteen-year relationship”: Confidential source.

22. Yetnikoff and Mottola:

23. “personal valet”: Emon quoted in “The Fall of Yetnikoff.”

24. “Mottola is a no-talent”: AI with Levy, July 30, 1988.

25. Yetnikoff and Grubman:

26. Basic details: “Spinning Out.”

27. “fail-safe mechanism”: Grubman made this comment to the manager of an artist client, who mentioned it to me in a not-for-attribution interview.

28. Yetnikoff and Geffen:

29. Basic details: Several of the above-mentioned articles discuss the bad blood between the two men, notably “Spinning Out” and “Doesn’t David Geffen Know the Eighties Are Over?” Both articles refer to Yetnikoff’s crack about Geffen’s bisexuality. As author Eric Pooley puts it in “Spinning Out,” Yetnikoff “said he wanted a girlfriend to take lessons from Geffen in the performance of a certain sex act; in exchange, he’d buy Geffen Records for $1 billion in Sony stock.” Author Jennet Conant is even more graphic in “Doesn’t David Geffen …,” noting that “according to industry insiders, while on the phone with a Geffen executive, Yetnikoff made a crack to the effect that he’d like his girlfriend to get lessons from Geffen in ‘sucking cock.’ ” Conant apparently refers to Yetnikoff’s phone call to Eric Eisner, which I learned about from a confidential source who spoke to both Yetnikoff and Eisner within hours of the call.

30. “I date men and I date women”: “David Is Goliath.”

31. Geffen and Jackson: Most of the details are from two confidential sources close to Jackson, and corroborated by several of the above-mentioned articles.

32. Geffen’s interest in luring Jackson from CBS: Discussed rather pointedly in “Spinning Out” and “Doesn’t David Geffen Know the Eighties Are Over?”

33. “Well, wait a second”: AI with Geffen, July 27, 1989.

34. Branca defends his negotiations for Jackson: AI with Branca, Mar. 8, 1991.

35. “Walter went crazy”: Quote from “The Fall of Yetnikoff.”

36. Spy story: “Who’s the Biggest Man in Hollywood?,” Apr. 1991.

37. Yetnikoff’s professional demise:

38. “events will prove”: Fred Goodman’s interview with Yetnikoff, August 21, 1990.

39. Ohga’s meeting with Yetnikoff: Confidential source; also cited in “The Fall of Yetnikoff.”

40. “He obviously already planned”: Confidential source.

41. “Ding dong”; “Walter slit his own throat”: “David is Goliath.”

42. Schulhof gets Sony Music chairman post: “Sony Taps Schulhof as Vice Chairman of Its U.S. Unit,” The Wall Street Journal, Dec. 14, 1990.

43. Negatives about Mottola: “Spinning Out.”

44. “Tommy has to look at himself”: Yetnikoff made this comment to a former CBS Records employee, who repeated it to me the following day.

45. Velvel: “Doesn’t David Geffen Know the Eighties Are Over?”


The following people not cited in the above notes generously made themselves available for interviews: Fred Ansis, Frank Barsalona, Bob Brundige, Bill Cataldo, Max Collins, Syd Connor, Betty Cordes, Herb Corsack, Al DeMarino, Joel Denver, Bob Feiden, Dizzy Gillespie, Michael Klenfner, Cal Mann, Leonard Marks, Tony Martell, Bhaskar Menon, Bob Pittman, Ed Rosenblatt, Peter Sarasohn, Paul Schindler, Ekke Schnabel, Bruce Schoen, Tom Silverman, Barbara Skydel, Jim Sotet, Mike Stewart, Frank Wohl.

ABOUT THE AUTHOR




Fredric Dannen, a contributing editor at Vanity Fair, was a co-recipient of the Overseas Press Club’s 1986 Morton Frank Award for business reporting from abroad. His articles have appeared in The New York Times, Institutional Investor, Rolling Stone, and Barron’s. He received the Ralph J. Gleason Music Book Award for Hit Men.
